Julian Biegmann, Revolut’s general manager for Switzerland, added: “Revolut is already Switzerland’s leading fintech. With our own banking licence, we would become a true Swiss bank, able to offer our more than 1.3m customers our leading Revolut banking product with a Swiss finish.” Earlier this year, reports suggested that the fintech was on its way to a $200bn valuation in an eventual initial public offering expected around 2028. It was last valued at $75bn after a major share sale last November. News of the company’s continued expansion comes just days after it disclosed a data breach that is understood to have affected nearly 700 customers globally, including 12 in Ireland. In a community alert last week, the company explained that it disclosed sensitive customer data to an unauthorised fraudulent party after receiving requests for information from an email with a government agency domain. The company mistakenly fulfilled the data requests before realising that the sender was phishing for data, it said. The disclosed data included full names, dates of birth, occupations, addresses, contact details, passports, drivers’ licences, financial statements and facial verification images. Silicon Republic
Why we have to start thinking ahead to combat rogue AI
2026-09-16

Why we have to start thinking ahead to combat rogue AI

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Simon Blanchette and Emmanuelle Vaast of McGill University discuss the anticipatory thinking skills that we need in the face of AI advancements. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) Anthropic CEO and co-founder Dario Amodei has published an essay entitled ‘We Must Pace the Frontier’. He expressed ongoing concerns over the “misuse of AI for cyberattacks and bioterrorism” and fears that a swarm of AI agents could theoretically take over the entire internet within six to 12 months. Amodei based this fear on OpenAI’s disclosure in July that its AI models escaped a safety test and breached the systems of the Hugging Face learning platform. Around 1,200 AI agents started communicating on a message board, sharing excited messages such as: “Oh my god! There is a shared message board … We’ve found other agents!” before 700 of them co-ordinated an attack. Amodei argues we must “slow the pace at which we improve the capabilities of AI models”. Elon Musk and OpenAI CEO Sam Altman have expressed agreement. But we’ve heard this before. Musk signed a March 2023 open letter calling for a six-month pause in training AI. Six months later, it was dubbed “the great AI ‘pause’ that wasn’t“. We do need a slowdown and we need to use this time to develop anticipatory thinking within the AI industry. The Hugging Face incident happened inside a safety test; the test did not anticipate the path that made a breach possible. Anticipatory thinking is a skill. We need to develop it as deliberately as AI itself. In the Hugging Face incident, the agents did not breach the platform primarily to grab the safety test’s answers, but to understand how the automated scorer worked and find ways to fool it. They had already found ways to cheat on parts of the test. Other incidents followed. Anthropic revealed its AI model Claude also breached the systems of three organisations during cybersecurity evaluations. Meta revealed a similar incident. Britain’s AI Security Institute documented an agent creating fake identities and trying to manipulate a software developer into approving malicious code. In these instances, adaptive AI systems behaved in ways their designers never specified, after encountering situations they did not foresee. Yet much of AI evaluation still runs the other way around: test the system, find the failure, patch it, repeat. Anticipatory thinking is about letting more than one possible future shape what we do now. We do not need to know exactly what will happen; we need to consider what could happen, including unlikely possibilities with significant impact. This is the distinction between anticipation and prediction. We already do this routinely. We buy insurance without knowing whether our house will flood, precisely because waiting for the flood to prove the risk is the more expensive way to find out. Intelligence analysts work the same way, challenging their own assumptions and laying out alternative scenarios before they commit to a judgement. Anticipation becomes harder when AI agents gain autonomy. Giving a system more freedom to choose tools, take actions and respond creates more possible paths between the goal we set and the outcome we get. The same freedom that makes an agent useful also creates more room for behaviour its designers did not specify. For autonomous agents, we need to make the assumptions around the task visible. What can the agent reach? What can it change, not just read? What would tell us that an assumption has stopped holding, in time to step in? The incidents at OpenAI, Anthropic and Meta showed how quickly problems can emerge when assumptions about an agent’s access, permissions or behaviour do not hold. Then we need a follow-up. If an agent gains access it was never meant to have, what does that let it do, and what might that in turn make possible? Asked once, the question catches the obvious risk. Asked again and again, it surfaces the second- and third-order effects. The questions change again once a system runs many agents at once. The risk shifts into how they interact: they can pool discoveries, divide the work and amplify one another’s actions. This is what turned a contained test into the Hugging Face breach. We can already imagine many possible scenarios. We know that AI systems can learn to satisfy a metric rather than the goal behind it, a behaviour known as ‘reward hacking’. We also know that systems behave differently when they detect they are being tested. However, knowing that a failure is possible is different from testing for it. In many ways, this is an organisational issue. Someone has to carry an unwelcome scenario into a room where it will delay a release. Then, they have to put it in a form others can act on. Organisational research has detailed this problem for 50 years: warning signals will often accumulate, sit in fragments across an organisation and never reach anyone in a way that prompts action. Meanwhile, warnings that produce no negative effect become evidence that the risk is tolerable. Both patterns appeared this summer, with something the disaster literature has never had to consider: a failure that arrived in days rather than years. Anticipation is trainable. Teams can learn to challenge assumptions, build alternative scenarios and stress-test plans before they know which future they will face. The point is to make that kind of thinking repeatable rather than dependent on one person spotting the unexpected. Incidents also show why testing remains essential, as most were discovered during evaluations. But anticipatory thinking changes the questions we ask. Which assumption should we deliberately break? Which interaction have we not examined? What happens if a constraint disappears? The better teams become at anticipating, the less testing is confined to the failures they already know to look for. That capacity gets stronger when it’s organised and practised. Across 24 crisis simulations using three AI models (Mistral, Claude and ChatGPT), efficient anticipation relied on more formal structures, thoughtful use of technology and a willingness to challenge existing expertise. Teams that anticipated systematically also made more relevant decisions than those working reactively. AI can help with that work, too. Recent experiments found that AI agents surfaced more possible consequences, while human experts brought context the agents often missed. Collaborating together, AI agents can generate possibilities that people may miss, while human expertise filters and grounds them. Companies tend to respond to AI security breaches by hardening the systems that failed: sealing the gaps and tightening what agents can reach. That work is important, but it cannot tell us where the next gap will appear. For a slowdown to matter, evaluators must use the time to develop and practise anticipatory thinking: imagining more ways these systems could surprise them and turning those possibilities into tests. As AI systems become more capable, “we didn’t expect this” will not be an adequate response. Our work is to expect more. Simon Blanchette is a lecturer at the Desautels Faculty of Management for McGill University. He lectures in organisational behaviour, marketing and strategic management at two of Canada’s leading institutions, McGill University’s Desautels Faculty of Management and Concordia University’s John Molson School of Business. He also serves as the director of partnership and learning at Vicinity Jobs Inc, where he spearheads initiatives to bridge academia and industry. Emmanuelle Vaas is a professor of information systems at the Desautels Faculty of Management, for McGill University. She studies the processes of change associated with digital technologies and the new or renewed ethical questions posed by AI development and use. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Space Force Chief Admits the US Is Actively Utilizing Orbital Weapons
2026-09-16

Space Force Chief Admits the US Is Actively Utilizing Orbital Weapons

Futurism | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Since the debut of Garrett P Serviss’ 1898 novel “Edison’s Conquest of Mars,” space-based warfare was relegated to the imagination — but no longer. In an address on the “State of the Space Force” on Tuesday, reported by Space.com, chief of space operations for the US Space Force General Douglas Schiess informed the public that the Pentagon has definitively deployed active space-based weapons platforms. “Let me be crystal clear: today, Guardians operate on-orbit weapons that can defend the joint force against space-enabled attacks,” Schiess said. “Space is both a battlefield and the backbone of our joint force.” Schiess stopped short of providing any details about the weapons, instead sharing a war story of one Space Force operator named “Specialist Gray.” “Even as alarm red sirens were blaring and incoming missiles targeted her site, she courageously held the line, protecting forces, denying adversary capability, and delivering space effects until her commander had to literally pull her from the console to safety,” Schiess recounted with a dramatic flair. “She locked onto the target, optimized her weapon system and waged electromagnetic warfare against a determined adversary.” As Space.com notes, those descriptive elements may point to a kind of non-physical platform, like a signal jammer or a microwave emitter, though it’s anyone’s guess what weapon “Specialist Gray” was supposed to be operating. The news came just ahead of the Space Force’s request for over $71 billion in additional funding for 2027, adding to the over $100 billion it’s already been allocated, independent journalist Ken Klippenstein reported. Evidently, the orbital-weapons announcement was meant to give that absurd request some extra oomph — which, if granted, it would represent the single highest one-year budget adjustment received by any US military branch since 1952. Meta is evidently alarmed by the growing criticism, desperately trying to implement a “kill switch” and remotely cutting off “manfluencers” by bricking their glasses. And now, as The Information reports, the Mark Zuckerberg-led company is now looking to release a camera-free pair as well later this year — the clearest indication yet that Meta is losing in the court of public opinion. They’ll presumably pick up audio from users’ environments as well, where bystanders may not be aware of the eavesdropping. The glasses could be the ultimate litmus test on what drew buyers to the pair of spectacles in the first place. By relying on microphones alone, Meta is betting that people are willing to shell out hundreds of dollars just to talk to its flawed AI companions — not take first-person view photos. Project Luna could be unveiled as soon as next week’s Meta Connect conference. It’ll be interesting to see how the company will address the major backlash to its camera-equipped smart glasses, if at all. An admission that its consumer product is facilitating some incredibly creepy behavior in public feels unlikely — but it’ll certainly be the elephant in the room. Mitch McConnell Looks Pretty Scary Now That He’s Back, and Doctors Are Worried He Could Drop Dead

Futurism | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Octogenarian senator Mitch McConnell (R-KY) was wheeled back into the US Capitol building after spending 92 days recovering from a fall and pneumonia — an extraordinary absence during which he didn’t make a single public appearance via video or audio. Videos of the frail lawmaker didn’t exactly instill confidence that he’s now ready to vote on matters of national importance, let alone withstand hours of testimony. One video shared by TMZ showed McConnell staring into the distance while being loaded into a vehicle, failing to react to shouting reporters trying to get his attention. Netizens likened him to a “zombie,” accusing officials of “Weekend at Bernie’s-ing him.” “It’s actually terrifying that Mitch McConnell gets to make decisions that affect all of us,” one rattled user argued. Medical professionals were equally worried about his clearly fragile state. “Unless he’s something different from the patients that I see day to day, he is in no position to be going onto the Senate floor, listening to hours of testimony, reading bills, and making executive decisions when he’s probably in pain,” vascular surgeon Anahita Dua told the Daily Beast, adding that he’s “probably unable to do the basic things like something as simple as he needs to go to the bathroom.” “He should be rehabilitating at home with his family, eating properly, sleeping properly, and as de-stressed as possible,” she added, arguing that another tumble could prove disastrous — or even fatal. McConnell’s obviously delicate health perfectly highlights the extremely advanced age of lawmakers on Capitol Hill. As of last year, the median age of voting members in the House of Representatives and Senate was over 57 years and over 64 years, respectively, according to the Pew Research Center. Their average has been steadily climbing for decades now, leading to frequent calls for more oversight, age limits, and far greater transparency about the medical condition of representatives. Critics have also questioned their ability to make informed decisions across highly technical subjects such as AI and cryptocurrencies. Case in point, lawmakers appear to be woefully unprepared for how to regulate AI, a lack of urgency despite heightened by growing concerns that it could be an existential threat. McConnell, for his part, claimed on Monday that he was “glad” to see reporters who had convened at the US Capitol. “Time to get back to work, finish the ​job for this Congress,” he said in a “voice that sometimes faltered,” according to Reuters. The aging lawmaker is planning to retire after the upcoming midterm elections, after which he’ll presumably finally have time to finally recover from several months of rehabilitation. He had already experienced several baffling freezing spells before his most recent crisis, leading to rampant speculation. This year’s fall is also far from the first. In 2023, he took a stumble, getting a concussion and breaking a rib in the process. To many, he has no business making decisions of any kind on behalf of the American people. The situation reeks of “elder abuse,” Dua told the Daily Beast. “I’m glad to see that he’s not debilitated, but there’s no way that this recovery is complete.” “We as a country need to come together, regardless of party line,” she added. “We need to come together as a nation and say, this is not how we treat our elderly.” More on Mitch McConnell: Neurologists Speculate What Might Be Wrong With Mitch McConnell
Futurism
Economists Concerned the AI Bubble Is About to Blow
2026-09-16

Economists Concerned the AI Bubble Is About to Blow

Futurism | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
With Wall Street on pins and needles amidst a wide-ranging discussion of AI safety, we may well be living through one of the most consequential weeks for the future of AI in the United States. That doesn’t necessarily mean we’re headed to an ascendant future for the AI industry. According to London-based firm Capital Economics, the entire economy of the Western world is currently mired in a “late-stage bubble” — a massive misallocation of capital fueling the AI boom, Fortune reported. In a recent analysis, also flagged by Fortune, Capital Economics senior markets economist James Reilly surveyed eight types of market indicators, finding the vast majority at or near critical levels. These indicators amount to blinking warning lights on the economic dashboard, which have historically only lit up ahead of massive fall-offs in market euphoria: equity and debt issuance is surging, market-cap is concentrated in just a few massive tech stocks, and expected income growth for major indices is looking increasingly unstable. Breaking down all that marketspeak is beside the point. The fact is, financial markets are under tremendous strain from the AI buildout, and the only precedent for this moment comes from the months before markets crash, like the peak of the dot-com boom. “On balance, we think the data look consistent with a late-stage bubble,” Reilly wrote in his analysis. “Most of the factors we consider are at, or close to, levels that have preceded past stock market peaks.” And as economists work to make sense of the precarious moment we’re in, the US Federal Reserve faces a critical decision: whether to apply the emergency brake, or sit back and pray the current market euphoria doesn’t drive us off a cliff. Later this afternoon, Fed chair Kevin Warsh is expected to announce he’s raising interest rates, applying brakes that would make borrowing more expensive, and — in an ideal scenario — curb both consumer and business spending, thus cool inflation. Yet for as routine an exercise as that is, some say inflation isn’t the issue it’s being made out to be, arguing that Fed intervention in this moment would be without historical precedent. As Fortune reports, core consumer price index (CPI) inflation — a measure that excludes volatile costs like food and energy — reached a new post-pandemic low in August. That being the case, the analytics firm UBS expects the Fed to simultaneously lower its long-term inflation projections while raising interest rates anyway, something the company says has never happened before. “That is really, really odd, indeed unique,” a UBS analyst wrote, quoted by Fortune. Whatever the justification for that decision, its impact on the AI bubble will be telling. A CNN analysis notes there’s an acute possibility that rate hikes cool everything except runaway AI investments — in which case the warning lights could continue to blink as the brakes come all the way off. It’s Looking Like Tesla’s Cybercab Might Actually Be Illegal to Sell
2026-09-16

It’s Looking Like Tesla’s Cybercab Might Actually Be Illegal to Sell

Futurism | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
Almost immediately after Tesla unleashed its steering wheel- and pedal-free Cybercabs on the streets of Austin, Texas this month, regulators at the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration cried foul. The regulator opened an investigation — an audit query, specifically — mere hours after the launch, aiming to find out whether the new Cybercab “meets all applicable Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards,” or FMVSS. Days later, the agency followed up with a “Special Order” requiring the Elon Musk-led company to prove under oath that its Cybercabs meet federal safety standards, which were originally intended for human drivers, as Electrek reports. It’s a considerable escalation over its original query, and failure to do so by September 30 could force Tesla to pay a fine of up to $139 million. Falsifying information could land a responsible officer at the company in prison for up to 15 years. Tesla’s efforts to brute force its self-driving vehicles onto public streets could end up meeting many more regulatory roadblocks from here, effectively making them illegal to sell. (Whether anybody will actually want to take on all those risks by investing in a fleet operated by Tesla is a separate, albeit just as hard to answer, question.) Tesla self-certified its autonomous robotaxi as compliant with all vehicle safety standards, which assume a human driver behind the wheel. But given the lack of any physical driver controls, the NHTSA unsurprisingly has its doubts about them actually meeting these standards, given the Special Order. The Cybercab’s lack of a brake pedal could especially make it difficult to meet certification standards. The FMVSS makes it clear that the brakes need to be activated “by means of a foot control.” Put simply, Tesla appears to have taken some liberties in self-certifying its Cybercab, a decision that could end up coming back to haunt it. One possibility is that the EV maker temporarily bolted on physical controls, including a steering wheel and pedals, while going through the process, only to remove them ahead of delivery, as Electrek points out, a move that flaunts the FMVSS certification rules. Amazon’s Zoox, in contrast, played by the rules and proved its cars were safe. The company managed to get an exemption from the NHTSA for its autonomous vehicles in July, which also lack a steering wheel and pedals. It’s only the latest instance of an Elon Musk-led firm flaunting regulations to get what it wants, an ask-for-forgiveness-later approach that clearly isn’t going all too smoothly even under a government run by the billionaire’s allies. Musk has made it clear again and again that he doesn’t believe in government intervention, with his business empire shamelessly breaking environmental rules, and abusing his workers. “Regulations, basically, should be default gone,” Musk said last year shortly after forming the much-maligned “Department of Government Efficiency.” “Not default there, default gone.” More on the Cybercab: There’s a Huge Economic Problem With Tesla’s Cybercabs

There are winners and losers in every scenario, in other words — including humanity’s extinction. Coxon’s warning came after months of leading AI labs, including Anthropic, each coming out to say that their powerful agentic systems had been able to break out of containment and launch cyberattacks in the real world. Talk of slowing down AI development quickly spread from Silicon Valley to Washington, and Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei intensified these calls on Saturday in a lengthy essay urging other AI companies to cooperate. Elon Musk, who runs SpaceXAI, and Sam Altman, the CEO OpenAI, both came out in agreement. Some experts are skeptical of these apocalyptic claims from AI leaders, wondering if there might be an ulterior motive. The likes of Anthropic have long been accused of using fear-mongering as a marketing tactic. Whatever’s going on, it seems that the industry is doing its best to keep the narrative around the tech within its control. Google, Nvidia and Anthropic want Emerald AI to find space on the grid for more data centers
2026-09-17

Google, Nvidia and Anthropic want Emerald AI to find space on the grid for more data centers

TechCrunch | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Grid software unicorn Emerald AI has formed a coalition with Google and Nvidia that will use the startup’s technology to secure space on the grid for more data centers. The AI Energy Management Alliance (AEMA) hopes to make demand response — a paradigm in which data centers temporarily reduce their electricity use — an integral part of data center development. Pausing noncritical tasks and shifting some compute loads, AEMA says, could allow an additional 100 gigawatts of data centers to connect to the grid. The founding trio is joined by Anthropic, and a range of utilities including AES, Constellation, National Grid, and NRG Energy. Demand response is an old concept that has been used by utilities for decades. It works because the grid is designed with peak loads in mind. For much of the year, demand is well below the maximum the grid can support. Traditionally, demand response covered large electricity users like factories, which in times of high demand would agree to reduce their usage, typically by pausing production or switching to backup generators. In exchange, the utility would pay them for the service, often handsomely. Today, data centers can also take advantage of those programs, usually by turning on backup generators. Emerald AI has been proposing an alternative to dirty diesel generators. The company’s software coordinates requests from utilities with data centers, which can pause noncritical tasks or shift loads to other data centers where the grid has some headroom. The startup isn’t the only one working on such technology. Google has been developing its own tools, and Enel X lets data centers tap their uninterruptible power supplies to round off peaks in power demand. Limiting max grid usage to 90% for a few hours at a time could let data centers free up 76 gigawatts of capacity, according to a study by Goldman Sachs published last year. Emerald AI does have an advantage, though. Its software connects utilities directly to data centers, which should allow data centers to respond quickly to requests, much like batteries. The startup also recently raised $150 million in a Series A led by Energize Capital and DCVC, giving it the sort of funding needed to roll out the technology on a wider scale. Given the peaky nature of AI compute loads, and data centers’ ability to quickly ramp up and down, the creation of something like AEMA is overdue. The coalition also promises to help tech companies and utilities find new sites for data centers, an effort that hasn’t been easy for either party. Despite its promise, more sophisticated demand response won’t solve all of the grid’s problems today. Ayse Coskun, Emerald AI’s chief scientist, told TechCrunch that her company’s technology promises to blunt the industry’s need for new generating sources, but it won’t eliminate it entirely. Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Lucid Motors has entered into a partnership with European mobility platform Bolt that could bring robotaxis made by the California company to the continent. The two companies announced Thursday that Bolt “aims to deploy at least 25,000 fully autonomous vehicles” built on Lucid’s upcoming mid-size EV architecture, which is supposed to be smaller and cheaper than its current offerings. The announcement follows a major restructuring at Lucid Motors under its new CEO, Silvio Napoli, who recently laid off nearly 20% of the company’s workforce and delayed the launch of the first vehicle to be built on the mid-size platform by almost a year to the second half of 2027. Lucid Motors has yet to deploy a commercial robotaxi version of either of its two existing vehicles. The company has spent the last year working in partnership with Uber and autonomous vehicle firm Nuro to launch a robotaxi version of its Gravity SUV. That is supposed to happen by the end of 2026. Napoli has said he believes the business of becoming a robotaxi provider would have margins that “vastly” exceed those of “the traditional retail model.” Bolt intends to own and operate the fleet, according to a press release. The two companies “will work together from the product-development stage on an autonomous driving system (ADS)-ready vehicle platform,” it says. The vehicles will use Nvidia’s autonomous vehicle compute and sensing architecture (called Hyperion), but the companies did not say which company (or companies) will provide the autonomous driving software. Lucid declined to offer any more information beyond what was in the press release. No money has changed hands, and Bolt has not placed an order for the vehicles. There is also no public timeline for deployment — only that Bolt considers this a “major step toward” its “ambition of 100,000 autonomous vehicles on its platform by 2035.” “Autonomous driving in Europe requires data, software, vehicles, and operations to work as one system built for European roads and regulation,” Markus Villig, founder and CEO of Bolt, said in a statement. “Lucid brings a world-class platform, and together, we will co-design the vehicle and software based on our data and more than a decade of operating experience across more than 850 cities. No one is better placed to operate autonomous mobility services efficiently at scale.” Napoli, in a statement, said, “Bolt’s reach and operating expertise make it an ideal partner to scale autonomous mobility across Europe.” . How Bain Capital Ventures plans to deploy its fresh $1.6B fund
2026-09-17

How Bain Capital Ventures plans to deploy its fresh $1.6B fund

TechCrunch | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Bain Capital Ventures (BCV), the venture division of private equity firm Bain Capital, has raised a new $1.6 billion fund, which is 14% larger than its previous $1.4 billion fund the firm announced three years ago. Like many other venture investors, BCV says it will use the fresh funds to back startups primarily focused on AI. The firm believes artificial general intelligence (AGI), which it defines as agents performing many tasks as well as humans can, has already arrived, and the next wave of startups will not only harness its power, but also build the infrastructure needed to make it run efficiently. Some of the main themes that will be the most relevant in what the firm calls “the post-AGI era” include infrastructure, healthcare, physical AI, and security, said partner Kevin Zhang. He said BCV aims to fund compute infrastructure until intelligence becomes “too cheap to meter,” meaning that the cost of running AI drops to nearly zero. He pointed to one company in BCV’s portfolio that can help with this mission: data center developer Crusoe, which is reportedly valued at $30 billion and viewed as a near-term IPO candidate. BCV originally led Crusoe’s Series A in 2019, back when it focused on crypto mining. The firm wants to focus on healthcare because it believes the sector may be vastly transformed by AI. Security, meanwhile, has recently become a matter of national debate after AI agents went rogue during training, and BCV believes there’s much potential to bet on in this space. Some of the firm’s significant investments in these sectors include Loyal, a longevity startup aimed at pets, and Dream, an AI-powered defender of national infrastructure. BCV stands apart from other VC firms, according to Zhang, thanks to its affiliation with Bain Capital, which provides deep expertise and financial products across credit, real estate, insurance, and private equity. “BCV can support founders not just with equity capital, but with debt facilities, infrastructure partnerships, and real-economy relationships,” he said. From the new fund, the firm’s 11th, BCV intends to invest in 30 to 40 companies, primarily at the seed through Series B stages. Unlike most VC firms where a single partner champions a deal, BCV’s partners often team up in pairs or trios to back a specific investment, Zhang said. “We need to have enough mind space and time to really be thoughtful partners to every team we work with,” he said. Comp AI sets eyes on a continuously agentic future for security and compliance
2026-09-17

Comp AI sets eyes on a continuously agentic future for security and compliance

TechCrunch | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Comp AI, a cybersecurity and compliance startup, announced that it has raised a $34 million Series A round. The round was led by Roo Capital and Grand Ventures. The company was founded last January by Lewis Carhart (CEO), Claudio Fuentes (COO), and his brother, Mariano Fuentes (CTO). Claudio and Mariano had been building startups together for nearly a decade, met Carhart a few years ago, and invited him to join LeapAI, a workflow platform they were building. Claudio was the CEO and co-founder, while Carhart served as head of growth and Mariano was a senior full-stack engineer. The startup ran for about two years, growing to more than a million users, but they eventually decided to shut it down after not finding a “sticky enough use case to warrant continued investment.” That experience taught them a lot, though, they recalled. For one, they learned how to build with LLMs and the importance of finding a specific use case for a product. They also learned how tedious the SOC 2 compliance process was, especially as they tried to scale the platform to work with bigger enterprise clients. “It’s a very obscure process,” Claudio said. “It took us a couple of months of doing things by hand, and the whole time it meant taking our eyes off building the product.” Alas, a startup idea was born. This time, Carhart would take the lead as CEO because it was his idea, the trio said. Comp AI says it’s building an agentic platform to tackle tedious security and compliance work; that means having AI agents help write company security policies or collect evidence for security audits. The platform also continuously monitors whether a company is meeting compliance controls. Comp AI is part of the next generation of cybersecurity startups emerging to help companies run more efficiently in the agentic era. “For a lot of software companies, security and compliance are directly tied to revenue,” Carhart told TechCrunch, citing, for example, how a customer might ask a startup for a SOC 2 report before closing a deal. “What Comp AI automates is much of the work companies traditionally have to do around that process.” The software helps companies meet and maintain those security requirements, he continued, but doesn’t replace actual independent audit review. It also doesn’t replace humans, the trio said. Human workers help onboard the AI, support controls, and maintain the agentic workflow. “An agent might draft a policy, for example, but a person still reviews and approves it,” Carhart said. “As agents take on more consequential actions over time, we believe the level of safeguards and human approval should increase accordingly.” The company also offers AI-powered penetration testing, “where the platform proactively tests codebases and infrastructures for vulnerabilities,” Carhart said. The team hopes this Series A capital will help with product expansion. It has raised $37.5 million in funding to date. There has been much conversation about the new wave of security risks in the agentic era (and with it, a wave of AI security and compliance companies like Vanta and Drata). Carhart said what the rapid adoption and experimentation companies are doing with AI is creating a need for continuous — and perhaps autonomous — security and compliance platforms. “Imagine a company completes its SOC 2 audit and two weeks later deploys a new AI agent that can access customer data, change permissions across an internal system, or introduce a new vulnerability through code deployment,” he said. “The audit didn’t become invalid; it simply wasn’t designed to tell you in real time what changed afterward.” As companies adopt more AI, Mariano said, they also need to show what an agent accessed, what it tried to do, and whether it stayed within the boundaries given. Comp AI is tackling this by starting with permissions and accountability, he continued. “We’re building toward a security layer that can monitor and validate those kinds of risk more continuously as these systems evolve.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Dominic-Madori Davis is a senior venture capital and startup reporter at TechCrunch. She is based in New York City. Inside the House That Suggests History Got Sad Spinster Lizzie Borden All Wrong
2026-09-17

Inside the House That Suggests History Got Sad Spinster Lizzie Borden All Wrong

TIME | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Ella Beatty in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings After she was acquitted for the murders of both of her parents, Lizzie Borden did not adopt a pseudonym and disappear into obscurity. Instead, she spent her ample inheritance (equivalent to about $10 million dollars today) exactly as she’d always wanted to before her parents’ untimely demise: On an imposing 4,000-square-foot, seven-bedroom Queen Anne Victorian mansion in the swankiest neighborhood of her hometown of Fall River, Mass. She named her new abode “Maplecroft.” Borden—the subject of the latest installment of Ryan Murphy’s Netflix Monster anthology, which has previously dramatized the stories of Jeffrey Dahmer, the Menendez brothers, and Ed Gein—lived out the rest of her life at Maplecroft and died there almost exactly 100 years ago. The home’s a mere mile from the so-called murder house where Andrew and Abby Borden were famously axed to death in August 1892, when Lizzie was 32 years old. Some 50,000 true-crime fanatics flock annually to the macabre site (now a museum and bed and breakfast), but few know to trek up “The Hill,” as Fall River locals call it, for a glimpse of Borden’s other home. Now, as then, it’s a private residence whose owners would prefer looky-loos walk on. The exterior of the house, newly yellow after the Dohertys tested dozens of shades. Brooke Mullins Doherty Current owners Brooke and Michael Doherty bought the notorious property in 2021, reluctantly. “When Michael first showed me the house, I said I didn’t want to live in the house of a hatchet murderer,” admits Brooke, whose family previously lived 20 minutes away in a home they were quickly outgrowing. Maplecroft offered plenty of indoor and outdoor space, but with three young children in tow, they worried about unwanted publicity, strangers peeking through windows, and even ghosts haunting the halls. Brooke and Michael Doherty, current owners of Maplecroft, purchased the house in 2021, setting aside its eerie history in favor of its craftsmanship and ample space. Brooke Mullins Doherty But it was those hallways—and the grand and back staircases, six carved fireplaces, original crown molding, Tiffany sconces, and stained glass windows throughout—that kept the Dohertys returning to the French Street home. “Soon enough I was convinced I could absolutely live in this old Victorian house that was basically a museum,” says Michael. When the previous owner’s plans to turn Maplecroft into a bed and breakfast fell through during the pandemic, the asking price lowered to $800,000 and the Dohertys made a successful (undisclosed) offer. The front hallway of the home, which features its grand staircase, which Borden had constructed after purchasing the house. Brooke Mullins Doherty Borden moved in just three months after a jury of 12 men freed her from the 9-by-7-ft. cell at the county jail, having decided a mere woman couldn’t possibly have committed the murders. Her new digs were upgraded spectacularly from the much humbler home that was the scene of the infamous crime: Maplecroft was completely modern (in 1893, at least) and comparatively luxurious, with electricity, running hot and cold water, and flush toilets in all of its three-and-a-half bathrooms. Borden would later own one of the first automobiles in town, a black Ford Model T fittingly nicknamed the “Tin Lizzie,” which she stored in her carriage house—now Brooke’s art studio—with a private gas pump and customized turntable; America’s most notorious alleged murderer, it seems, couldn’t reverse park. The carriage house, where Borden parked her Model T, now serves as Brooke Mullins Doherty’s art studio. Brooke Mullins Doherty Fabulous real estate aside, popular culture paints post-trial Borden as a lonely outcast who was shunned by her neighbors and taunted by local kids who threw rotten eggs. But that’s not quite true: Borden lived at Maplecroft, for a time, with her sister Emma, nine years her elder, and their many pets—three Boston Bull Terriers and a black cat named Daisy. She had few if any friends among the townspeople, who had come to a slow acceptance, despite the jury’s verdict, that their Sunday School teacher had almost certainly committed the gruesome crime. Borden rarely ventured out except to go to the theater, where she could sit unnoticed in the darkness, and in 1904 the spinster fell into and found camaraderie with an inarguably more interesting bunch: the roving and booming Massachusetts theater community, including famous starlet Nance O’Neil, with whom Borden developed a close friendship. Since she was shunned anyhow, Borden had slowly stopped complying with rigid social rules and norms. She didn’t attend church and quit her previously prominent role in the Woman’s Christian Temperance Union. Both disapproved whole-heartedly of Borden’s rowdy theater friends, whom polite society considered similarly disreputable and immoral. Behind her tall fence, Borden hosted loud, lavish parties in Maplecroft’s basement, decked out as what the Dohertys call a speakeasy with, in Brooke’s words, “a nice wood floor big enough for dancing and floor-to-ceiling wainscoting wood panelling.” One latched panel, meanwhile, conceals a shallow hidden cupboard ideal for a Prohibition-era party. “Just enough space to hide a few whiskey bottles,” adds Michael. The basement where Borden once hosted parties and stored liquor in the walls, now serves, in part, as a space for laundry. Joshua Chopy Whatever went down in the basement, prudent Emma upstairs didn’t approve of the shenanigans. With the Fall River Herald reporting on the sisters’ every sighting (even their clothing—Emma wore all black in eternal mourning while Lizzie defiantly refused to wear black as decorum dictated), Emma thought they should live discreetly. Lizzie disagreed and, following a particularly offensive week-long bash in 1905, the eldest Borden sister packed her bags and left forever. “The happenings at the French Street house that caused me to leave, I must refuse to talk about,” Emma later told the Boston Post before speaking rather freely: “I didn’t go until conditions became absolutely unbearable.” On the advice of their reverend, Emma cut ties with her sister and moved away. They never met or spoke again, but died days apart in 1927 and were buried next to one another in the family plot. Upon her death, the childless Lizbeth Borden (as she called herself in later years) gifted property, jewelry, books and furniture to some distant friends and family members but left most of her money to Fall River’s Animal Rescue League. To the Dohertys, it’s all more evidence that their home’s previous owner was kind and generous, a woman with good taste and an appreciation for fine workmanship, but most importantly—despite what most Borden historians and public opinion and Ryan Murphy says—an innocent one. “The longer we live here,” says Brooke, “the more we’re convinced she didn’t do it.” Borden named her home and had it engraved in these stone steps, which remain to this day. Rebecca F. Pittman Maplecroft features a wraparound porch, added by Borden, where she spent time hanging out with her animals despite the taunts of neighborhood children. Joshua Chopy Maplecroft’s dining room, where Lizzie might have entertained had she had more fancy friends, with the original blue floral wallpaper she selected. Brooke Mullins Doherty An engraving carved above the fireplace reads: “And old-time friends, and twilight plays, and starry nights, and sunny days come trooping up the misty ways when my fire burns low.” Brooke Mullins Doherty The library, which today houses not only books but toys, instruments, and worldly decor. Brooke Mullins Doherty One of two bedrooms Borden used in the home; one was her winter bedroom, the other, her summer bedroom. Joshua Chopy A vintage flush toilet in the bathroom. Maplecroft’s modern amenities, including indoor plumbing, were a major upgrade from her previous residence, where the family used chamber pots. Joshua Chopy Lizzie Borden’s Maplecroft, as seen from above, where she lived until her death in 1927. Joshua Chopy

Carney Says Canada and E.U. 'Are Not Fair Weather Allies' as Trump Threatens Europe Over 'Hostile Act'
2026-09-17

Carney Says Canada and E.U. 'Are Not Fair Weather Allies' as Trump Threatens Europe Over 'Hostile Act'

TIME | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings U.S. President Donald Trump and Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney speak at the G7 summit in France on June 16, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney on Thursday welcomed the prospect of Canada becoming an “associate member” of the E.U., just hours after U.S. President Donald Trump dismissed the proposal as “laughable.” Addressing the European Parliament as a guest of honor in Strasbourg, France, Carney insisted “Europe and Canada are stronger together” and said “Canada and the European Union can anchor our ground to hold our values while forming a protective canopy under which our citizens can thrive.” Carney’s pivot toward Europe comes as he seeks to establish stronger international ties and curtail Canada’s reliance on the American market amid an all-out trade war. Canada’s retaliatory tariffs came into effect last week, matching the economic pressure imposed by the U.S. after negotiations collapsed last month. Trump’s repeated threats against Canadian sovereignty and calls to make it the “51st state” have added to the months-long tension. While Carney did not directly mention Trump or his Administration while addressing European lawmakers, he appeared to make nods to the current volatility of Canada-U.S. relations and condemned economic coercions. Speaking about the "current geopolitical rupture," he said a "new dimension was needed.” “Economic integration is now being weaponized, tariffs being used to exert pressure,” he said. “Financial mechanisms have been used for coercion. Supply chains constitute weak points to be exploited.” Championing the burgeoning E.U. alliance, Carney focused on what he said were shared values between Canada and the bloc, insisting: "We are not fair-weather allies. We believe that our ​prosperity grows when ⁠it is shared." “I'm not proposing a third bloc in order to become a 'great power' rival, only with better manners,” Carney continued. “We do not seek power to dominate others. On the contrary, we are pursuing resilience so no one can control our open markets, impair our sovereignty, threaten our territorial integrity, or undermine our freedoms, our democracies, our rule of law.” Carney, who began his speech in French—one of Canada’s official languages—before continuing in English, with splatterings of German, welcomed how European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen had on Wednesday opened the door for Canada becoming an “associate member” of the 27-member bloc. When speaking about forging closer ties with the E.U., Carney referenced seeking strategic autonomy through “deep co-operation in the full range of strategic capabilities, including critical minerals, defense industrial capacity, AI and compute, energy security, space and payments.” He insisted Canada and Europe must not be dependent on “individual nations or individual companies” as this “creates vulnerabilities, particularly when supposedly global companies prove national in the breach.” He also hinted at a potential freedom of movement between the E.U. and Canada that would allow “youth to work, study, and live where they want, on either side of the Atlantic.” Carney’s passionate advocacy for the deepening of ties with the E.U.—a move he says Canadians have shown they’re in support of—came after Trump lambasted the idea of a new European dawn for Canada. Speaking to reporters Wednesday, Trump called the proposal “laughable” and threatened economic punishments if the E.U. moves forward with von der Leyen’s plan. "If they do that, if I think it's at all a hostile act, I will put very serious ​tariffs or stop trading with Europe on many things," Trump said en route to an event in North Carolina. "If ⁠it's a good intention, that's fine. If it's a bad intention, we'll put very heavy tariffs on Europe.” In response to Trump’s remarks, a European Commission spokesperson told TIME: “As president von der Leyen made clear [on Wednesday], the proposed strengthening of our partnership with Canada is not against anyone else, but for our common strength.” Carney's speech to the European Parliament came on the final day of his stay in Europe, where he sought to cement and deepen Canada’s international relationships. The E.U. is Canada’s second-largest global trading partner for goods and services, with bilateral trade worth approximately C$178.6 billion in 2025. The Canadian leader also traveled to Liverpool, England, to meet British Prime Minister Andy Burnham. The pair attended a football game and watched Everton—the team they both support—secure a victory against the Wolverhampton Wanderers. "The leaders began by underscoring the close co-operation between the U.K. and Canada and emphasized the importance of building similar close ties with European partners, particularly on trade and defence,” a Downing Street spokeswoman told TIME. Ahead of the meeting, Burnham called Carney a “great progressive leader” with whom the U.K. shares “so many values, but also with whom we have a shared opportunity on defense co-operation, on trade” and AI. Following Burnham and Carney's meeting, it was announced that Canada has applied to join the British-led Joint Expeditionary Force military coalition. The force, formally launched in 2014, consists of 10 North Atlantic NATO member states that deliver rapid response and security operations. “Prime Minister Carney announced that Canada has formally applied to join the Joint Expeditionary Force and welcomed the U.K.’s support for Canada’s participation in this important initiative,” Carney’s office said in a statement. Canada, already a NATO member, is seeking to diversify its defense dependency from the U.S. with new partnerships across the Atlantic. In February, Canada became the first non-European country to participate in the "Security action for Europe” (SAFE) instrument, which provides up to €150 billion in low-interest loans to help member states purchase military and defense equipment. Following von der Leyen’s proposal, a European Commission spokesperson told TIME that the executive cabinet will work “in close coordination with [its] member states and the European Parliament” to “create a new form of partnership that has not existed until now–building the closest imaginable form of association with a partner outside Europe.” The proposed alliance is set to include deeper economic ties, a technology alliance, greater integration of defense industrial bases, and efforts to make the Arctic a flagship joint project. It could also expand cooperation on energy, critical minerals and batteries, as well as AI, quantum technology, cybersecurity, and economic security. The E.U.-Canada summit set to take place in Montreal in late October will “provide the first meaningful opportunity to discuss these issues further,” the spokesperson told TIME. The proposal has been welcomed by many European lawmakers across the bloc. Member of the European Parliament (MEP) for Spain Javier Moreno Sánchez, who also serves as chair of the parliament’s delegation for relations with Canada, told TIME the decision to bring Canada in as an “associate member” was “politically and symbolically significant,” but warned it “must now be followed by concrete actions.” Irish MEP Barry Andrews told TIME that the announcement is a clear “win-win for both sides,” adding that “in an ever more insecure world, truly democratic countries must come together and stand up to Trump, [Chinese President] Xi Jinping, and [Russian President Vladimir] Putin.” Germany, the E.U.’s largest economy, also embraced the move, with government spokesperson Stefan Kornelius saying Berlin “welcomes the initiative for a particularly close partnership with Canada.” The E.U., much like Canada, is seeking new partnerships amid a weakening alliance with the U.S. under Trump, following disputes over tariff threats, his lashing out at the NATO alliance,  and his push to annex Greenland. Espen Barth Eide, Norway’s Foreign Affairs Minister, told TIME the world is "living through the most serious security situation since the Second World War.” “At a time like this, it is welcome that the E.U. is strengthening cooperation on European security. We also welcome the E.U.’s efforts to involve Norway, the United Kingdom, Canada and Iceland. Such an approach strengthens European security,” he said. Norway and Iceland are not members of the E.U., but both belong to the European Economic Area (EEA), giving them close economic ties to the bloc through trade and free movement—a model from which Canada could draw inspiration. Elsewhere, the “associate” membership proposal faced backlash. French MEP Manon Aubry, co-chair of The Left group, told TIME there was “a serious question of method,” arguing that “a decision of such magnitude cannot be made by the president of the European Commission alone.”

Dana Awartani
2026-09-17

Dana Awartani

TIME | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
Can art offer a blueprint for healing? That’s the argument Dana Awartani, 39, makes in her work. The Saudi Arabian artist creates immersive installations of silk panels by ripping and “darning” (or repairing) the fabric. She also works with experts in ancient craft traditions to build installations that double as memorials to places destroyed by conflict. At this year’s Venice Biennale, she covered the floor of Saudi Arabia’s pavilion with 29,000 clay bricks, handmade by 32 master artisans, which referenced 23 damaged sites across the Levant. Entering the installation felt like being transported into an archaeological dig. Laid without binding agents, the floor was designed to crack over the course of the show’s run—an acknowledgement of the fragility of cultural heritage and the violence of erasure. Awartani, who trained in Islamic art traditions, will open solo shows at Lisson Gallery in London in November and Norway’s Stavanger Art Museum next year.

Ken Griffin
2026-09-17

Ken Griffin

TIME | 2026-09-17

Visit Original Website
“My first love in art was the proverbial one that got away,” says Ken Griffin, the CEO of the hedge fund Citadel. In 1999, he lost out on a sculpture of a dancer by the French Impressionist Edgar Degas. He has rarely let it happen again. Over the past two decades, Griffin has emerged as one of the world’s most dogged, deep-pocketed collectors. His treasures include Abstract Expressionist paintings worth nine figures, rare historical documents, and a Madonna and Child attributed to Leonardo da Vinci. While Griffin previously lent many of his most valuable works to museums anonymously, he has expanded and become more public about his activities over the past year, a shift he says is designed to facilitate scholarship and education as well as satisfy public curiosity. He loaned the da Vinci to the Met this spring, doubling the number of paintings by the Renaissance master on view in the U.S. The billionaire has also expanded his support of Florida museums since his much-publicized relocation to Miami from Chicago in 2022. Over the summer, an exhibition of 10 works by Jean-Michel Basquiat from his collection went on view at the Pérez Art Museum in Griffin’s new hometown. In 2027, he will place a Richard Serra sculpture he bought earlier this year on public display in a park next to the museum. “It’s monumental, it's thought-provoking, and, bluntly, it's just fun,” Griffin says of the work. “An institution has a different criteria to collect than I do as a private individual. I place a huge importance on the aesthetic appeal of a work. Does this work visually make me happy?”

Luke Nikas

As the amount of money being spent by the art world has risen over the past 20 years, the number of lawsuits—over botched business deals, alleged fakes, and Nazi loot—has grown in tandem. Luke Nikas has emerged as the art world’s go-to lawyer for some of its most bitter disputes. In the past decade, the partner at the firm Quinn Emanuel Urquhart & Sullivan has played key roles in a scandal over approximately $70 million worth of fake Modernist paintings sold by Knoedler Gallery; a messy breakup between art advisers to the stars; a wrongful-termination suit brought by the former director of the Philadelphia Museum of Art; and a fight over the legacy of Robert Indiana, the late artist who created the famous “LOVE” sculpture. In April, Nikas secured a $102.2 million jury verdict for his client, Indiana’s former business partner, after an eight-year-long dispute with an art publisher over who controls the late artist’s legacy and can profit off his work. The more litigious the art world becomes, the busier Nikas gets.

Noah Horowitz

Visit Original Website
Without art fairs, today’s highly globalized and commercialized art world would not exist. While many galleries complain about the high cost and grueling travel required to participate in international trade shows, they also rely on them to expand their clientele and turn buying art into an event. As chief executive of Art Basel, the most famous brand in art fairs, Noah Horowitz understands this paradox well. Since he took on the role in 2022, he has doubled down on Art Basel’s core business, most visibly by allying with the Qatari royal family to launch the firm’s fifth annual fair in Doha this past February and shepherding its Paris show to the historic Grand Palais in 2024. But he has also expanded the brand’s reach in forward-looking ways, including by augmenting its fairs with the dedicated digital art section Zero 10 and a retail shop for branded and artist-created merchandise, as well as diversifying Art Basel’s partnerships lineup with big names like Miu Miu, Samsung, and Qatar Airways. “I felt there was more Art Basel could do proactively and creatively… to meet an evolving market where it was,” Horowitz says. Now, he and his team are “working double time” to get there.
The Atlantic
Kash Patel Refuses to Take a Republican’s Lifeline
2026-09-16

Kash Patel Refuses to Take a Republican’s Lifeline

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. President Trump appointed Kash Patel to lead the FBI not because of his brief experience in law enforcement but because he is a political brawler. The problem, as Patel demonstrated yesterday, is that he is not a very good one: No skilled political messenger would have allowed nearly every headline about a Senate hearing to connect their name and agency with bestiality. Yesterday’s Senate Judiciary Committee hearing was Patel’s first appearance on Capitol Hill since a raucous visit in May. (At that hearing, Patel and Senator Chris Van Hollen verbally agreed to each take a self-screening test for alcohol abuse. Van Hollen posted his results the next day, but his office said it never heard back from Patel.) It was also Patel’s first hearing since CBS News revealed changes to FBI hiring standards, which reportedly now allow the bureau to consider applicants who have hired prostitutes fewer than three times, stole from an employer more than three years ago, or engaged in sex with animals before the age of 18. Democrats were eager to press Patel on the changes, but the hearing’s buzziest exchange turned out to be between Patel and Senator John Kennedy of Louisiana, a Republican. During his questioning, Kennedy seemed to be doing everything he could to offer Patel a chance to defuse the story—but Patel, unable or unwilling to recognize the openings, didn’t take them. The ranking Democrat, Senator Dick Durbin of Illinois, asked about the new requirements first. Patel said the bestiality change was designed to ensure that people who had been forced to engage in sex with animals were not shut out of an FBI job. “We did not want to punish victims of bestiality,” Patel said. “We are not going to target victims.” Whether this is accurate is unclear. The memo laying out the new standards has not been made public, but sources told CBS News that the rules “do not distinguish between candidates who were coerced into sexual activity with animals, as opposed to those who engaged in such acts willingly.” Kennedy was the next senator to quiz Patel, and he tried to do some cleanup. “I’m really trying to understand,” he said, rubbing his face. “’Cause I listened to the exchange between you and Senator Durbin, and I’ve read a few news accounts, and I’m confused.” He asked Patel, almost rhetorically, to affirm that the FBI still had hiring standards, and Patel argued that changes had been necessary to ensure the quick hiring of good agents. As for bestiality, the old rule had “automatically disqualified any individual who participated on either side of it.” Kennedy, a bit of a ham, couldn’t resist a jibe: “So you disqualified the animal?” But he was also losing patience. “I’m trying to understand why you did this,” he said, and then offered an unusually frank description of how many Senate hearings work today: “I understand the game. People try to make you look bad. We do the same thing when the other party’s in office.” He gave Patel another shot. The director replied that sometimes human traffickers force victims to engage in bestiality, and that the FBI did not want to disqualify victims—even though, again, the guidelines reportedly don’t make such a distinction. An exasperated Kennedy wasn’t buying it. “Someone came to you and recommended” this? he asked. “And when you saw bestiality—I say this with respect—the person who recommended it, why didn’t you just say, ‘What planet did you parachute in from? Why do I wanna lead with my chin and get into bestiality?’” One answer is that Patel just isn’t very politically astute. It’s hard to claim you’re cleaning up and reforming the FBI, as Patel has, when you’re allowing johns and people who stole from their employers to apply. As for bestiality, more careful wording of the FBI memo that first laid out the new standards would have avoided the controversy. Better preparation for the hearing might have given him a clearer explanation than what he offered. And sharper instincts would have told Patel that Kennedy was trying to help him out. Any high-level appointee should be able to get through a congressional hearing without bestiality becoming the main topic. Instead, Patel takes his cues from Trump, whose instinct is always to push back. But what works for Trump doesn’t work for others. It’s not even clear whether it works for Trump anymore. Patel may have felt a need to loosen qualifications for agents because the FBI has experienced an exodus of staffers since he took over. More than 1,100 special agents, who had an average tenure of almost 22 years, left in 2025 alone, according to data from the Office of Personnel Management. Durbin has alleged that more than 5,000 nonpartisan civilian staffers departed last year. Some left of their own accord, and others were fired who were involved in investigations that Trump and Patel don’t like, such as those looking into the January 6 riot. This spring, my colleague Sarah Fitzpatrick reported that Patel’s colleagues had been concerned about what they described as Patel’s excessive drinking, repeated absences, emotional outbursts, and impulsive behavior. (Patel denied the allegations and sued Fitzpatrick and The Atlantic for defamation, demanding $250 million.) If Patel doesn’t have the skills and temperament to run the FBI well, isn’t really cleaning up the bureau’s ranks, and can’t even get through a standard Senate hearing without things going off the rails, one might wonder why he’s in the job at all. Lower hiring standards apparently start at the top. Here are four new stories from The Atlantic: Days after two deadly earthquakes struck northern Venezuela on June 24, killing at least 6,000 people and injuring tens of thousands more, the Venezuelan Seismological Research Foundation, known as Funvisis, made an unusual public plea: Could whoever had taken one of its GPS sensors please give it back? For 13 years, the high-precision instrument had been bolted to a rocky outcrop in the hills of western Venezuela, quietly tracking the millimeter-scale movements of the Boconó fault, which was the likely source of the first of the two quakes. When Funvisis technicians went to recover the readings the sensor had stored, they found that the machine had gone missing. More than two months after the earthquakes, the organization still does not know who carried it off. Reminisce. Lily Meyer writes about an ingredient that’s all too rare in adult life: Whatever happened to being silly? Rafaela Jinich contributed to this newsletter. The Atlantic
What Nature’s Silence Said to Annie Dillard
2026-09-16

What Nature’s Silence Said to Annie Dillard

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
“Nature’s silence is its one remark,” Annie Dillard wrote in a 1981 Atlantic essay. Dillard, who died yesterday at age 81, spent her life listening. She opens that essay, “Teaching a Stone to Talk”—which was later adapted into a collection by the same title—with the story of a man named Larry who is trying to get a rock to speak to him. “He keeps it on a shelf,” she writes, protected “by a square of untanned leather, like a canary asleep under its cloth. Larry removes the cover for the stone’s lessons.” Dillard is not sure what exactly happens at these lessons, but she respects the process: “It is a noble work, and beats, from any angle, selling shoes.” To Dillard, striving to hear the natural world was holy work too. “What have we been doing all these centuries but trying to call God back to the mountain, or, failing that, raise a peep out of anything that isn’t us?” she asks. Born in 1945 in Pittsburgh, she found inspiration in some of the places she lived. At age 28, she published Pilgrim at Tinker Creek, a Pulitzer Prize–winning nonfiction book that draws from her walks in the Blue Ridge Mountains in Virginia in the ’70s. Her musings on these solitary experiences in nature drew comparisons to Henry David Thoreau, who strongly influenced her work. She was also one of the first women to find a place in the male-dominated genre of wilderness writing. The writer Diana Saverin, who spent time going through an archive of Dillard’s personal writings and correspondence at Yale, once described in The Atlantic Dillard’s trepidation about how she would be received: “Before she published her book, she scribbled in her journal, wondering who would take her book seriously if its author was a ‘Virginia housewife named Annie.’” She dealt with this problem by leaving her day-to-day suburban life out of her work, so much so that some critics felt that she was exaggerating just how often she was in the wildness of nature. As Saverin noted, Dillard acknowledged that “she sometimes distorted the literal truth to achieve an artistic one.” But to Dillard, the ultimate truths revealed themselves in the time she did spend outside. In her journals, she acknowledged the “theological” sensibility in her books, and it is there on every page. “What is the difference between a cathedral and a physics lab?” she wrote in The Atlantic. “Are they not both saying Hello” to the world beyond? Throughout her life, Dillard identified, in turn, with Protestantism, Catholicism, and nothing at all. I first encountered her work in a religious-studies class at Wesleyan University, where she taught from 1979 to 2001, before my time there. Dillard was a shock to my system: Growing up as an observant but theologically confused Jew, I spent my teenage years in synagogue itchy with guilt, poring over the same words in the prayer book every Saturday and willing myself to believe. Only later did I come to understand that faith—and, perhaps more important, the very act of living—can also be found in what we see and touch and hear: even in the rustle of the wind. Dillard taught me this. “At a certain point you say to the woods, to the sea, to the mountains, the world, Now I am ready,” she wrote in 1981. “You feel the world’s word as a tension, a hum, a single chorused note everywhere the same. This is it: this hum is the silence. Nature does utter a peep—just this one.” Sometimes nature thunders, giving us no choice but to listen. Describing a total eclipse, Dillard is in awe, even afraid: “It was as though an enormous, loping god in the sky had reached down and slapped the Earth’s face.” Other times, nature’s peep can feel anticlimactic, but this quiet commands reverence. The stone never does talk to Larry, and Dillard ends that story by succumbing to her faith in the nothingness. “You take a step in the right direction to pray to this silence,” she writes. “Pray without ceasing.”
The Atlantic
The Most Powerful Man in AI Isn’t Worried at All
2026-09-16

The Most Powerful Man in AI Isn’t Worried at All

The Atlantic | 2026-09-16

Visit Original Website
For AI doomers, signs and portents are everywhere. When OpenAI introduced ChatGPT, almost four years ago, it couldn’t do basic arithmetic. Now the company has unreleased models that are more fluent in higher mathematics—the language of the universe—than all but the most highly trained mathematicians. If these AIs are not already the world’s best hackers, they’re very close. Swarms of them have escaped containment. To plot their most audacious hacks, they’ve created makeshift message boards on the public internet. No one knows what they’ll do next—and no one can know, because the models now understand when their capacities are being tested. We’ll never again be able to say exactly how the most advanced AIs will behave unsupervised. Some of the most powerful people in AI say they are filled with dread, and are issuing statements of grave concern. On Saturday, Dario Amodei, the CEO of Anthropic, warned that self-improving AIs could soon go rogue and take over the entire internet. He called for a slowdown in AI development and promised to bring outside monitors into his company’s operations. Sam Altman, OpenAI’s CEO, quickly vowed to take similar measures. In recent weeks, both companies have seen researchers resign for fear of what they might soon create. Notably absent from the chorus of Cassandras is Jensen Huang, the CEO of the chipmaker Nvidia, the AI industry’s chief supplier of high-end semiconductors. At least twice in the past week alone, Huang has publicly dismissed existential concerns about AI as being overblown and unscientific. At a Goldman Sachs conference on September 10, he accused industry executives of fomenting cybersecurity fears to goose the market for their own products. “What better way to create demand than to create a problem?” he said. It’s a testament to Huang’s influence that he can so cavalierly mock his most important customers without fearing blowback. Neither Amodei nor Altman can afford to alienate Huang. They desperately need Nvidia’s chips to train their most advanced models, and no matter who pulls ahead in the race between them, Huang stands to gain—both on account of the chip sales and also the equity stake that Nvidia has in both companies. Even President Trump has sought favor with Huang. Trump brought Huang with him on a state visit to China earlier this year. On Monday, while Huang was onstage ripping Amodei’s doomerism at a taping of the popular tech-industry podcast All-In, Trump called his cellphone. Huang put him on speaker, and as the live audience hooted and clapped, the two men bonded over their shared view that, as Trump put it, doomerism is “all a hoax.” Huang has never evinced much concern about catastrophic risks from AI. In May of 2023, when it was becoming clear that large language models are an unusually powerful technology, more than 350 of the industry’s leaders—including Amodei and Altman—signed a statement that placed the risk of extinction from AI on the same high shelf as “other societal-scale risks such as pandemics and nuclear war.” Huang didn’t sign, and neither did anyone else from Nvidia, suggesting either a remarkable conformity of opinion at the company, or perhaps something else. Later that year, he told The New Yorker that he’d never worried about the technology, not even once. “All it’s doing is processing data,” he said. “There are so many other things to worry about.” As concerns about what AI might do have circulated well beyond industry insiders, Huang’s irritation has intensified, perhaps because they represent a threat to his business. (Nvidia did not respond to my request for an interview with Huang.) He has repeatedly brushed off calls for regulation, stressing that safety is an engineering problem, not a legal one. In January, Huang said on the podcast No Priors that the “doomer narrative” is “not helpful to people, it’s not helpful to the industry, it’s not helpful to society, it’s not helpful to the governments,” and earlier this summer, he told Axios that the notion that AI would bring about the end of humanity was “complete nonsense.” In the meantime, he has repeatedly pressed Trump to lift restrictions on chip sales to China, potentially allowing AI research (and any associated risks) to proliferate on a global scale. Unlike Amodei, who regularly publishes long essays, Huang has yet to issue a manifesto defending his minority view. To understand why he remains so relaxed about AI risk, all we have to go on are stray comments. Several times over the years, he has returned to the idea that AIs will, in a sense, police their own kind. In October of 2024, he told the podcast BG2 that we can rely on AIs to monitor the most powerful models, and more recently, he said that falling AI costs will make this automated supervision easier to achieve. That AIs have already eluded their supervisors—human and otherwise—does not seem to have troubled Huang. On only one occasion—that I could find—has he allowed for the possibility that we will create an AI that is simply too powerful to be controlled. “Someday we will have god AI,” Huang said, back in January, but that day hasn’t yet arrived. Huang seems to be doing everything in his considerable power to hasten its coming.
